The workflow measures the physical imperfections of a specific device: how quickly its qubits relax and dephase, and how often its gates and readout fail. An ideal simulator has none of these imperfections, so there is nothing to characterize. b0:["$","p",null,{"children":"In this run, the refreshed properties changed some or all of the selected qubits at every size, and the refreshed layouts gave a higher mean fidelity for seven of the eight GHZ states. The largest gain was for the 25-qubit state, where the mean fidelity increased from 0.629 to 0.694, and the refreshed layout scored higher in every trial for the 5-, 10-, 20-, 25-, 35-, and 40-qubit states. Not every size improved: for the 15-qubit state the refreshed layout was slightly worse (0.813 compared with 0.821), and for the 30-qubit state the difference (0.596 compared with 0.591) is within the variation between trials.","className":"group paragraph-el text-text-primary mx-0"}]
b1:["$","p",null,{"children":"Refreshing properties does not guarantee better results. How much it helps depends on how far the device has drifted since its last calibration, and the characterization is itself a noisy snapshot: a qubit or gate that happens to measure well can be selected even if it is not actually better. Shot noise, further drift between characterization and execution, and differences in characterization methodology also affect the comparison. Use the measured change and its variation to decide whether refreshing is useful for your workload.","className":"group paragraph-el text-text-primary mx-0"}]
